Trading Analysis Report: AVGO¶

Generated: 2026-08-07 23:21:37

I. Analyst Team Reports¶

Market Analyst¶

Now I have all the data needed. Let me compile the comprehensive analysis.

AVGO (Broadcom Inc.) — Comprehensive Technical Analysis Report¶

Date: August 7, 2026

1. Executive Summary¶

AVGO closed at $427.76 on August 7, 2026, capping a dramatic ~15.5% rally over just seven trading sessions (from $370.32 on July 29 to $427.76 on August 7). The stock is now trading above all major moving averages and has broken above its Bollinger Upper Band, signaling a powerful short-term momentum thrust. However, several indicators flash caution: the ADX remains remarkably low at 16.05 (well below the 25 threshold for a confirmed trend), the TD-9 Sequential shows a daily sell-setup count of -7 (approaching the exhaustion level of 9), and the price is stretched ~$22 above its 10 EMA. The structural picture remains bullish (SuperTrend UP on all three timeframes), but the rally's sustainability is in question.

2. Price Action Context¶

The verified OHLCV data reveals a highly volatile period for AVGO:

  • Early June spike-and-crash: The stock surged to an intraday high of $494.22 on June 3, only to collapse to $385.12 by June 5 — a ~22% decline in two sessions. This established a pattern of extreme volatility around this stock.
  • June–July consolidation: After the crash, AVGO traded in a choppy $360–$400 range for nearly two months, with no clear directional conviction.
  • July 29 low: The stock bottomed at $370.32 (close), marking the launch point of the current rally.
  • August breakout: The decisive move came on August 4, when AVGO gapped from $392.23 to $418.16 (a +6.6% single-session jump on ~29.6M volume), then continued higher to $427.76 by August 7.

The current close of $427.76 sits 16.7% above the 200 SMA ($366.59) and 8.3% above the 50 SMA ($394.83), confirming a strong recovery from the July lows but also raising the question of whether the move is overextended.

3. Indicator-by-Indicator Analysis¶

3.1 — Moving Averages: close_10_ema & close_200_sma¶

Metric Value Signal
10 EMA (Aug 7) $405.67 Price is $22.09 above — stretched
200 SMA (Aug 7) $366.59 Price is $61.17 above — strong long-term uptrend
10 EMA trajectory Rising from $381.83 (Jul 29) → $405.67 (Aug 7) Sharp acceleration

Interpretation: The 10 EMA has surged ~$24 in seven sessions, reflecting the intensity of the current rally. However, the closing price sitting $22 above the 10 EMA is a significant deviation — historically, such gaps tend to mean-revert as price either pauses or pulls back to reconnect with the moving average. The 200 SMA at $366.59 confirms the long-term secular uptrend remains intact, and the stock is comfortably above this strategic benchmark. The wide spread between the 10 EMA ($406) and 200 SMA ($367) — nearly $39 — underscores the strength of the broader uptrend but also the potential for a short-term pullback without violating the structural bull case.

Actionable insight: Traders looking for entry should watch for a pullback toward the $405–$410 zone (10 EMA area) as a potential buying opportunity, rather than chasing at current elevated levels.

3.2 — MACD¶

Metric Value Signal
MACD line (Aug 7) +7.61 Strongly positive, rising rapidly
MACD signal (Aug 7) +2.24 Bullish crossover confirmed
MACD histogram (Aug 7) +5.36 Expanding — momentum accelerating
Crossover date ~Aug 3–4 MACD crossed above zero and signal line

Interpretation: The MACD provides one of the most compelling bullish signals in the current setup. On July 29, the MACD was at -2.94 (bearish). By August 3, it had turned positive (-0.84 → crossing zero), and by August 7 it reached +7.61 — a swing of over 10 points in just seven sessions. The histogram at +5.36 is not just positive but expanding, indicating that bullish momentum is still accelerating rather than fading. The MACD crossed above its signal line around August 3–4, generating a textbook buy signal.

The velocity of this MACD turnaround is notable. Going from -9.03 (July 8) to +7.61 (August 7) represents one of the sharpest MACD reversals in the lookback period, suggesting institutional buying pressure behind the move.

Caution: Such rapid MACD expansions can be followed by equally rapid deceleration. Watch for the histogram to stop expanding (flatten) as an early warning of momentum loss.

3.3 — RSI (Relative Strength Index)¶

Metric Value Signal
RSI (Aug 7) 64.86 Bullish, approaching overbought
RSI (Jul 29) 43.37 Neutral-bearish at rally start
RSI trajectory Rising steadily +21.5 points in 7 sessions

Interpretation: RSI at 64.86 is in bullish territory but has not yet reached the 70 overbought threshold. This leaves some room for the rally to continue before traditional overbought signals trigger. The rise from 43.37 to 64.86 in seven sessions is steep but not parabolic — during the early June spike, RSI likely exceeded 70 before the crash, so the current reading is comparatively measured.

Critically, there is no bearish divergence visible: both price and RSI are making higher highs, confirming the momentum behind the move. However, RSI is rising at approximately 3 points per session; at this rate, it would reach 70 within ~2 sessions, at which point overbought conditions would become a factor.

Actionable insight: The absence of divergence and sub-70 reading supports the bullish case in the very near term. However, traders should prepare for overbought conditions to materialize early next week if the rally continues at its current pace.

3.4 — ADX (Average Directional Index)¶

Metric Value Signal
ADX (Aug 7) 16.05 Below 20 — no established trend
ADX (Jul 8) 20.77 Was stronger, then deteriorated
ADX trajectory Rising from 10.77 (Aug 3) → 16.05 (Aug 7) Beginning to recover

Interpretation: This is the most cautionary signal in the entire analysis. Despite a 15.5% rally in seven sessions, the ADX sits at just 16.05 — well below the 25 threshold that confirms a tradable trend and even below 20, which flags a range-bound market. This apparent contradiction requires careful interpretation:

  1. ADX is a lagging indicator: It measures the sustained directional movement over 14 periods. A seven-session rally, no matter how sharp, takes time to register in ADX. The rise from 10.77 (Aug 3) to 16.05 (Aug 7) shows ADX is beginning to respond.
  2. The June–July chop context: For nearly two months, AVGO traded in a range-bound fashion ($360–$400), which suppressed ADX. The current rally is the first sustained directional move since then, but ADX has not yet "caught up."
  3. Implication: The low ADX suggests that trend-following strategies may not yet be reliable. The rally could still be a V-shaped bounce within a larger range rather than the start of a new sustained uptrend. If ADX continues rising and crosses 20–25 in the coming sessions, it would validate the trend.

Actionable insight: Do not treat this as a confirmed trending market yet. Use tighter stops and smaller position sizes until ADX crosses above 20–25. The SuperTrend data (below) provides additional context on trend structure.

3.5 — Bollinger Upper Band (boll_ub)¶

Metric Value Signal
Upper Band (Aug 7) $425.40 Close ($427.76) is above the band
Upper Band (Aug 3) $404.42 Band was much tighter 4 sessions ago
Band expansion +$20.98 in 4 sessions Volatility expanding rapidly
Bollinger Middle (Aug 7) $391.86 Price is $35.90 above the mean
Bollinger Lower Band (Aug 7) $358.32 Wide band spread = high volatility

Interpretation: The closing price of $427.76 has broken above the Bollinger Upper Band at $425.40 — a classic breakout signal. However, this can be interpreted two ways:

  1. Bullish breakout: When price closes above the upper band during a momentum surge, it can signal the start of a trend continuation, especially if the band is expanding (which it is — from $404 to $425 in four sessions).
  2. Overbought warning: Closing above the upper band can also indicate that the price has moved too far, too fast, and a mean reversion back toward the middle band ($391.86) is likely.

The key differentiator is volume and follow-through. The August 4 gap occurred on ~29.6M shares (well above the ~17M average), suggesting genuine buying interest. However, volume has declined in subsequent sessions (16.9M, 13.9M, 14.7M), which may indicate the initial burst of demand is fading.

The Bollinger Band Width (upper - lower = $67.08) is notably wide, reflecting the elevated volatility from the June crash and subsequent recovery. This means the bands will be slower to contract, potentially allowing the price to "ride the band" higher in a strong trend.

Actionable insight: A close back inside the band (below $425) would be an early warning of momentum fade. Conversely, sustained closes above the upper band with expanding volume would confirm a genuine breakout.

3.6 — SuperTrend (Multi-Timeframe)¶

Timeframe Direction Trailing Stop Distance from Close
Tier 1 — Weekly (primary) UP $334.85 +27.75%
Tier 2 — Monthly (regime) UP $265.48 +61.13%
Tier 3 — Daily (entry) UP $376.43 +13.64%

Interpretation: SuperTrend confirms a bullish trend alignment across all three timeframes — weekly, monthly, and daily are all UP. This is the strongest structural confirmation in the analysis and provides a clear framework for risk management:

  • The weekly stop at $334.85 is the primary trend-defining level. A weekly close below this would flip the primary trend to DOWN. At +27.75% away, there is substantial cushion.
  • The monthly stop at $265.48 reflects the long-term regime, which has been bullish since well before the current rally. The +61.13% distance indicates the secular uptrend is deeply entrenched.
  • The daily stop at $376.43 is the most tactically relevant level. A daily close below $376.43 would flip the daily SuperTrend to DOWN, signaling short-term trend exhaustion. This level coincides roughly with the July 29 low ($370.32) and the 200 SMA ($366.59), creating a confluence of support.

Actionable insight: The daily SuperTrend stop at $376.43 serves as the ideal hard stop for any long position initiated at current levels. This provides a defined risk of ~$51 per share (~12% downside) from the current close. The multi-timeframe alignment supports maintaining a long bias until the daily stop is breached.

3.7 — TD-9 Sequential (Multi-Timeframe)¶

Timeframe Count Setup Interpretation
Tier 1 — Weekly -3 Sell-setup (3 of 9) Early stage, no immediate concern
Tier 2 — Monthly -5 Sell-setup (5 of 9) Building, monitor closely
Tier 3 — Daily -7 Sell-setup (7 of 9) Approaching exhaustion

Interpretation: The TD-9 Sequential provides the second major cautionary signal alongside the low ADX. All three timeframes are building sell-setups (negative counts), meaning the DeMark framework is flagging this rally as potentially overextended:

  • The daily count at -7 is the most immediately relevant. A sell-setup completes at -9, which would trigger a reversal watch. At -7, the count is 2 sessions away from completion (assuming the rally continues). This means that if AVGO continues higher for two more sessions, the daily TD-9 would flash a formal exhaustion signal.
  • The monthly count at -5 is more concerning from a structural perspective. A monthly sell-setup reaching 9 would suggest a broader trend exhaustion, not just a short-term pullback. At -5, it's past the halfway mark but still has room.
  • The weekly count at -3 is early and not yet a concern. Since the weekly timeframe carries the highest weight, the fact that it's only at -3 provides some comfort that the primary trend is not yet exhausted.

Critical nuance: TD-9 counts can reset if the price fails to make consecutive higher closes. A pullback or consolidation day could reset the daily count, which would actually be healthy for the rally's longevity. The danger scenario is a straight-line push higher that completes the count at 9 without any pause.

Actionable insight: If the daily count reaches -9 (potentially by August 11), traders should tighten stops significantly or consider taking partial profits. A pullback that resets the count would be a healthier setup for continuation.

Bullish Confluence¶

Bearish / Cautionary Confluence¶

Declining volume after Aug 4 gap (29.6M → 14.7M) Price Data

The Key Tension¶

The core tension in this setup is between strong short-term momentum (MACD, RSI, price action) and weak trend confirmation + approaching exhaustion (ADX, TD-9). The SuperTrend provides the tiebreaker from a structural perspective — all timeframes are UP, supporting a bullish bias. However, the low ADX and high TD-9 count suggest that the current leg of the rally may be nearing a short-term exhaustion point, even if the broader trend remains intact.

5. Scenario Analysis¶

Scenario A: Bullish Continuation (~40% probability)¶

  • Price continues higher, closing above the Bollinger Upper Band with sustained volume
  • RSI pushes through 70 but holds (strong trends can keep RSI elevated)
  • ADX begins rising sharply, crossing 20 within 3–5 sessions
  • TD-9 daily count resets due to a consolidation day before reaching 9
  • Target: $440–$450 (measured move from the breakout)

Scenario B: Pullback / Consolidation (~45% probability)¶

  • Price fails to hold above the Bollinger Upper Band ($425.40)
  • Pullback toward the 10 EMA ($405–$410) or Bollinger middle ($391.86)
  • RSI retreats from 65 toward 50–55
  • TD-9 daily count may reset, setting up a healthier continuation
  • ADX remains low, confirming range-bound behavior
  • This would be a buy-the-dip opportunity if SuperTrend daily stop ($376.43) holds

Scenario C: Trend Reversal (~15% probability)¶

  • TD-9 daily completes at 9, followed by sharp selling
  • Price breaks below the daily SuperTrend stop ($376.43)
  • MACD histogram contracts and crosses negative
  • Volume spikes on selling (similar to the June 4 crash pattern)
  • Target: $360–$370 (200 SMA and prior lows)

6. Trading Recommendations¶

For Momentum / Swing Traders¶

  • Current stance: Hold existing longs but do not add at current levels
  • Entry zone for new positions: $405–$410 (10 EMA pullback) or $392–$396 (Bollinger middle / 50 SMA)
  • Hard stop: $376.43 (daily SuperTrend stop) — a close below this invalidates the short-term trend
  • Profit-taking zone: $440–$450 if the rally extends; scale out if TD-9 reaches 9

For Trend Followers¶

  • Bias: Bullish (SuperTrend UP on all timeframes)
  • Key level: Weekly SuperTrend stop at $334.85 is the trend-defining level
  • Position sizing: Reduce size given ADX below 20 — the trend is not yet confirmed
  • Add condition: Wait for ADX to cross above 25 before increasing exposure

For Risk Management¶

  • ATR: 16.60 — use this for dynamic stop placement (e.g., 2× ATR = ~$33 stop from entry)
  • Position size: Given the volatility history (June crash was ~22% in 2 days), keep individual position risk to no more than 1–2% of portfolio

9. Final Assessment¶

AVGO is in a short-term momentum thrust within a structurally bullish framework. The SuperTrend alignment across all timeframes and the price position above the 200 SMA provide a solid bullish foundation. The MACD and RSI confirm strong near-term momentum. However, the low ADX (16.05) and TD-9 daily sell-setup at -7 are significant warning signs that the current rally leg may be nearing exhaustion. The price is stretched above both the 10 EMA and the Bollinger Upper Band, increasing the probability of a short-term pullback or consolidation.

The optimal strategy is to hold existing long positions with a stop at the daily SuperTrend level ($376.43) while awaiting either (a) a pullback to the $405–$410 zone for adding to positions, or (b) ADX confirmation above 25 to validate the trend. Chasing the rally at $428 carries unfavorable risk/reward given the proximity to TD-9 exhaustion.

FINAL TRANSACTION PROPOSAL: HOLD

Rationale: The structural trend is bullish (SuperTrend UP on all timeframes, price above 200 SMA), but the combination of low ADX (16.05), TD-9 daily at -7 (approaching exhaustion), price stretched above the 10 EMA and Bollinger Upper Band, and declining volume makes this an unfavorable entry point for new longs. Existing long positions should be maintained with a stop at $376.43 (daily SuperTrend). New entries should wait for a pullback to the $405–$410 zone (10 EMA) or for ADX to confirm trend strength above 25.

1.2 "Broadcom Will Survive the AI Bubble, But Everyone Else Won't"¶

  • Barchart published a piece framing AVGO as the ultimate AI survivor — a company whose diversified semiconductor franchise (custom AI ASICs, networking, VMware software) gives it structural advantages even if the broader AI hype cycle cools. This narrative is significant for traders because it positions AVGO as a defensive AI play — one that benefits from AI capex but is not solely dependent on it.

1.3 Featured Across Multiple "Top Picks" Lists¶

  • AVGO was highlighted in "3 AI Infrastructure Stocks That Could Make You Rich in August" (24/7 Wall St.), "5 High ROE Stocks to Buy" (Zacks), and "Beyond NVIDIA and GPUs: The Next AI & Quantum Winners Leading 2026" (Zacks). This breadth of positive analyst coverage suggests strong consensus bullish sentiment.

1.4 Competitor Dynamics: AMD and Intel¶

  • AMD dropped 6% post-Q2 earnings and acquired an AI chip start-up in an effort to close the gap with Nvidia. AMD's post-earnings weakness could drive rotation into AVGO as investors seek AI semiconductor exposure with less earnings risk.
  • Intel named Dean Jarnac as Chief Sales Officer, signaling continued strategic reshuffling. Intel's ongoing struggles reinforce AVGO's competitive positioning in networking and custom silicon.

2. Global Macro & Market Context¶

2.1 Equity Markets at Record Highs¶

  • The S&P 500 ended at a record high this week, clocking its strongest week since April. Notably, this rally was driven by poor jobs data that calmed fears of rate hikes — a "bad news is good news" dynamic. This is a critical context for AVGO: in a risk-on environment with cooling labor data, growth-oriented semiconductor stocks tend to outperform.

2.2 Tariff and Trade Policy Developments¶

  • Companies are claiming billions in tariff refunds months after Trump floated $2,000 checks. This signals that tariff policy remains in flux, with potential retroactive changes. For AVGO, which has significant exposure to global semiconductor supply chains (manufacturing in Asia, sales globally), tariff developments are a key tail risk.
  • The US-China tariff agreement by August 31 has only a 6% implied probability on Polymarket, down 9.5 percentage points week-over-week. This declining probability suggests trade tensions may persist, which could weigh on semiconductor names with China exposure.

2.3 Consumer and Corporate Signals¶

  • Walmart reset store operations after a shopping cart risk, and Kroger made pricing moves — signals that consumer-facing companies are navigating operational challenges. Trade Desk's downbeat guidance was flagged by Wedbush as indicating both macro and structural issues, a potential caution flag for advertising/tech spending.
  • Constellation Energy signed 920 MW of new power deals including a Walmart PPA — reinforcing the AI data center power demand thesis that benefits AVGO's networking and infrastructure franchises.

Upside Catalysts¶

  3. Competitor weakness: AMD post-earnings selloff and Intel disarray may drive rotation into AVGO.
  4. VMware integration: Software revenue synergies could drive margin expansion and multiple re-rating.

Downside Risks¶

  1. US-China trade escalation: 6% probability of near-term resolution (and falling) means tariffs remain an overhang. Any new export controls on semiconductors could directly impact AVGO.
  2. AI bubble correction: If AI capex slows, AVGO's premium valuation could compress. However, the Barchart article argues AVGO is better positioned than peers to weather this.
  3. Higher-for-longer rates: 86% probability of no rate cuts in 2026 keeps discount rates elevated for growth stocks.
  4. Valuation risk: With AVGO outperforming the market and featured across multiple "buy" lists, sentiment may be overly crowded.
  5. Macro data surprise: The market is rallying on weak jobs data, but further labor deterioration could eventually shift the narrative from "soft landing" to "hard landing."

Insight: Net debt has declined from $57.8B to $45.3B over four quarters — a $12.5B reduction (21.6%). At the current pace of ~$3B/quarter in FCF available for debt reduction (after dividends), Broadcom could reduce net debt to ~$30B within 18 months. Interest expense is ~$776M/quarter ($3.1B annualized), which is very manageable relative to $34.7B in EBITDA.

8. Key Growth Drivers & Catalysts¶

  1. AI-Driven Semiconductor Demand: Broadcom is a key supplier of custom AI accelerators (XPUs) and networking silicon (Tomahawk, Jericho) for hyperscale data centers. The 48% YoY revenue growth in Q2 FY2026 likely reflects significant AI-related revenue acceleration.

  2. VMware Integration & Monetization: VMware integration is yielding operational leverage — gross margins are expanding, and the software segment is contributing high-margin recurring revenue. Cross-selling opportunities across Broadcom's enterprise customer base remain substantial.

  3. Deleveraging Cycle: As debt is paid down, interest expense will decline, providing a direct tailwind to earnings. Each $10B in debt reduction saves approximately $350-450M annually in interest.

  4. Dividend Growth: Dividends have grown from $7.03B (FY2022) to $11.14B (FY2025), a 16.6% CAGR. The current yield of 0.62% is modest, but the absolute payout growth signals management confidence.

  5. Share Repurchases: With FCF accelerating and debt moderating, buyback capacity is increasing. Q1 FY2026 saw $7.85B in repurchases — a reacceleration.

9. Risk Factors¶

  1. High Goodwill/Intangibles: $97.8B in goodwill and $28.3B in intangibles represent 70% of total assets. Any impairment (particularly if VMware underperforms) could result in significant write-downs.

  2. Customer Concentration: Broadcom's semiconductor business has historically had significant customer concentration among a few hyperscalers. Loss of a major customer could materially impact revenue.

  3. Cyclicality: Semiconductor demand is cyclical. While AI demand is currently strong, any slowdown in data center spending could impact growth.

  4. Regulatory/Antitrust: Broadcom's aggressive M&A strategy has attracted regulatory scrutiny. Future acquisitions may face heightened antitrust review.

  5. Negative Tangible Book Value: While improving, the negative tangible book value (-$38.4B) could concern value-oriented investors.

  6. Elevated TTM P/E: At ~70x TTM earnings, any disappointment in growth could lead to sharp multiple compression. However, the forward P/E of ~22x provides a more reasonable valuation floor.

10. Summary & Trader Takeaways¶

Bull Case: - Revenue growing ~48% YoY with expanding gross margins (69.5%) - FCF generation accelerating to $10B+/quarter with <1% CapEx intensity - Aggressive deleveraging reducing net debt by ~$3B/quarter - Forward P/E of ~22x with PEG of 0.47 — growth not fully priced - VMware integration delivering operational leverage - AI tailwind providing multi-year secular growth

Bear Case: - TTM P/E of ~70x leaves room for multiple compression if growth disappoints - $97.8B goodwill creates balance sheet risk - Negative tangible book value - Interest expense of $3.1B/year still a meaningful drag - Semiconductor cyclicality risk - High beta (1.47) amplifies market volatility

Overall Assessment: Broadcom's fundamentals are exceptionally strong and improving. The company is in the "harvest phase" post-VMware, with accelerating revenue, expanding margins, surging free cash flow, and rapid deleveraging. The forward valuation metrics (P/E ~22x, PEG 0.47) suggest the stock is attractively valued relative to its growth trajectory, particularly given the AI-driven secular tailwinds. The primary risks are execution-related (VMware integration) and cyclical (semiconductor demand), but the current data trajectory is firmly positive.

II. Research Team Decision¶

Bull Researcher¶

Bull Analyst: # The Bull Case for Broadcom (AVGO): An AI Infrastructure Juggernaut at a Reasonable Price

Let me cut right to the chase: AVGO is the most compelling risk-reward proposition in the semiconductor space right now, and the bears are fixating on short-term technical noise while missing an extraordinary fundamental story unfolding in real time.

The Fundamental Powerhouse Hiding in Plain Sight¶

The bears will point to the TTM P/E of ~70 and scream "overvalued!" But that's lazy analysis. Let me walk you through what's actually happening:

Q2 FY2026 revenue came in at $22.19 billion — up nearly 48% year-over-year. Let that sink in. This isn't a small-cap growth stock — this is a $2 trillion mega-cap growing like a startup. And that growth isn't decelerating; it's accelerating. Q1 FY2026 was $19.31B, and Q2 jumped to $22.19B sequentially. The trajectory is unmistakable.

Now here's the critical valuation insight the bears miss: The TTM P/E of 70 is massively distorted by VMware acquisition-related amortization charges. The forward P/E is just 21.95, and the PEG ratio sits at 0.47 — anything below 1.0 signals undervaluation relative to growth. You're looking at a company growing EPS at 85% YoY (Q2 FY2026: $1.91 vs. $1.03) trading at a forward multiple that value investors would envy.

The AI Infrastructure Moat Is Deeper Than Bears Realize¶

Broadcom isn't just a AI play — it's the diversified AI infrastructure play. Let me break down why the competitive positioning is nearly unassailable:

  1. Custom AI ASICs: Broadcom partners with Google (TPU) and Meta (MTIA) on custom silicon. As hyperscalers seek alternatives to expensive GPUs, Broadcom is positioned to benefit from the shift — not be disrupted by it.

  2. Networking Dominance: Every AI data center needs Tomahawk and Jericho switches. This is the ultimate picks-and-shovels play. You don't need to pick the AI winner; you just need data centers to keep being built.

  3. VMware Software Franchise: The acquisition is now delivering operational leverage. Gross margins expanded from 67.9% (Q2 FY2025) to 69.5% (Q2 FY2026). Software recurring revenue provides a valuation floor that pure semiconductor plays don't have.

The Barchart headline this week said it perfectly: "Broadcom Will Survive the AI Bubble, But Everyone Else Won't." That's not hype — it's a structural reality. AVGO has diversified end markets, recurring software revenue, and multiple AI revenue streams. If AI capex cools, AVGO has a cushion. If it accelerates, AVGO is perfectly positioned.

The Cash Flow Story Is Extraordinary¶

Here's where the bear thesis really falls apart. Let's talk about what AVGO is generating:

  • Free Cash Flow (TTM): $27.2 billion with a ~42% FCF margin
  • Q2 FY2026 FCF: $10.26 billion — up 60% from $6.41B a year ago
  • CapEx intensity: under 1% of revenue — this is an asset-light, IP-driven business

What are they doing with that cash? - Net debt reduced from $57.8B to $45.3B in four quarters — a $12.5B reduction - Dividends growing at a 16.6% CAGR (FY2022: $7.03B → FY2025: $11.14B) - Buybacks reaccelerating: $7.85B in Q1 FY2026 alone

This isn't a company stretching to justify its valuation. It's a cash-printing machine that's rapidly deleveraging while simultaneously returning capital to shareholders. The balance sheet is strengthening every quarter.

The Macro Backdrop Is Supportive¶

Let me address the macro picture because bears love to invoke macro risk:

  • S&P 500 at record highs — risk-on environment favors growth/semis
  • US recession probability: just 8% (and fell 5pp this week) — the soft-landing scenario is the base case
  • Japan recession probability dropped 21.5pp to 32% — positive for Asia supply chain stability
  • AMD dropped 6% post-earnings — competitor weakness drives rotation into AVGO
  • Intel is in disarray — reshuffling leadership while AVGO consolidates market share

The only real macro tail risk is US-China trade escalation, and even that has just a 6% implied probability of near-term resolution (meaning tensions persist but aren't escalating dramatically).

Countering the Bear Technical Arguments¶

Now let me address the technical concerns the bears will inevitably raise:

"ADX is only 16 — no confirmed trend!"¶

ADX is a lagging indicator. It measures 14-period directional movement. After two months of consolidation ($360-$400 range), of course ADX is suppressed. But look at the trajectory: it's already rising from 10.77 to 16.05 in four sessions. The trend is forming before our eyes. More importantly, SuperTrend is UP on all three timeframes — daily, weekly, and monthly. The structural trend alignment is bullish. ADX will follow.

"TD-9 is at -7 — exhaustion approaching!"¶

This is the most overblown bear argument. TD-9 counts can reset with a single consolidation day. In fact, a pullback to the $405-$410 zone (10 EMA) would be healthy — it would reset the exhaustion count, reconnect price with the moving average, and set up a more sustainable continuation. The bears are treating a potential two-session countdown as inevitable when it's entirely conditional on straight-line appreciation.

"Price is stretched above the 10 EMA and Bollinger Upper Band!"¶

Stretched? Yes. Problematic? Let me contextualize. The stock is 16.7% above the 200 SMA — that's a sign of a strong secular uptrend, not a bubble. The 10 EMA gap will close either through a minor pullback or through the EMA catching up (it's already surging from $381.83 to $405.67 in seven sessions). And the Bollinger Upper Band breakout on August 4 came on 29.6M shares — nearly double the 17M average. That's genuine institutional buying, not a low-volume fakeout.

"But what about the June crash from $494 to $370?"¶

Different context entirely. The June spike was a parabolic blowoff that pushed RSI well above 70. The current rally has RSI at 64.86 — bullish with room to run. There's no bearish divergence. The MACD histogram is expanding at +5.36, not contracting. Momentum is still accelerating.

The Sentiment Tailwind¶

Both institutional news and retail sentiment are aligned bullishly:

  • 93% of labeled StockTwits messages are bullish (14 bull vs. 1 bear)
  • Multiple analyst "top picks" features (Zacks, 24/7 Wall St., Barchart)
  • The dominant narrative themes — custom-silicon moat, AI infrastructure leadership, earnings catalyst ahead — are fundamentally grounded, not speculative hype
  • Even neutral observers note AVGO's relative outperformance in a weak semiconductor tape

When sentiment aligns with fundamentals, that's not a crowded trade — that's confirmation.

My Bottom Line¶

The bear case for AVGO relies on: 1. Short-term technical indicators that are either lagging (ADX), conditional (TD-9), or normalizing (10 EMA gap) 2. A TTM P/E that ignores acquisition accounting distortions 3. Macro risks that prediction markets assign low probabilities

The bull case rests on: 1. 48% YoY revenue growth at a $2T market cap 2. Forward P/E of 22x with a PEG of 0.47 3. $27B+ in annual FCF with under 1% CapEx intensity 4. Rapid deleveraging — net debt down $12.5B in four quarters 5. Expanding margins — gross margin up to 69.5%, operating margin ~49% 6. AI infrastructure moat spanning custom ASICs, networking, and software 7. SuperTrend UP on all timeframes with structural support at $376.43 (daily) and $334.85 (weekly)

The optimal strategy is clear: Hold existing longs with a stop at $376.43, and use any pullback to the $405-$410 zone as a buying opportunity. The risk/reward at current levels favors accumulation over caution — the fundamentals are simply too strong to bet against.

The bears are trading the chart. The bulls are trading the business. I know which side I want to be on. Bull Analyst: # The Bull Rebuttal: Why the Bear's "House of Cards" Is Built on Sand

I appreciate my colleague's thorough bear case — it's well-constructed and raises legitimate questions worth addressing. But here's the fundamental problem with the bear thesis: it's built on a series of "what ifs" stacked on top of each other, each presented as more probable than it actually is, while systematically discounting the most powerful fundamental momentum in the semiconductor industry.

Let me go point by point and show you why the bear's "reality check" is actually the distorted view.

1. The "Acquisition-Inflated Growth" Argument: Math Doesn't Lie, But the Bear's Does¶

The bear claims the 48% YoY revenue growth is "largely the product of lapping partial VMware contributions." This is factually incorrect, and here's why:

VMware closed in November 2023. That means FY2024 (ending October 2024) already included ~11 months of VMware revenue. By Q2 FY2025 (April 2025), Broadcom was already lapping a full quarter of VMware contribution. The bear's claim that Q2 FY2026 is "the first full quarter that included a complete year-over-year VMware comparison" is wrong — that anniversary effect was in Q2 FY2025, not Q2 FY2026.

The real tell is sequential growth. Q1 FY2026 revenue was $19.31B. Q2 FY2026 revenue was $22.19B. That's $2.88 billion in sequential quarterly growth — a 14.9% sequential increase. M&A anniversary effects show up in YoY comparisons, not sequential ones. You don't get 15% sequential growth from acquisition accounting. You get it from selling more product.

Let's also look at the margin trajectory. If this were just acquisition accounting rolling forward, gross margins would be stable — not expanding from 67.9% to 69.5% YoY. Margin expansion during a period of supposed "acquisition inflation" is the signature of genuine operational leverage and product mix improvement, not accounting artifacts.

The bear asks "what's the organic growth rate ex-VMware?" Here's a more useful question: What other semiconductor company is growing sequential revenue by 15% in a single quarter? The answer is: none of them. NVIDIA's sequential growth has been decelerating. AMD just missed and dropped 6%. Intel is reshuffling leadership because they can't grow. AVGO is the only game in town showing this trajectory.

2. The Forward EPS "Fantasy Math": The Bear Doesn't Understand Acquisition Accounting¶

The bear does some quick math: "TTM EPS is $6.12, forward EPS is $19.49, that's 218% growth — fantasy!" This is the most embarrassing argument in the entire bear case, and I'll explain why.

The TTM EPS of $6.12 is artificially depressed by VMware acquisition-related charges. In FY2024, Broadcom recorded: - $3.24 billion in amortization of acquired intangibles - $1.53 billion in restructuring and M&A charges

These are non-cash accounting charges that flow through the income statement and crush GAAP EPS, but they do not reflect the economic earning power of the business. They're artifacts of purchase accounting — the process of allocating the acquisition price to intangible assets and amortizing them over their useful lives.

Here's the reality: Q2 FY2026 net income was $9.31 billion. Annualize that: ~$37.2 billion. On ~4.68B shares, that's ~$7.95 per share annualized from the most recent quarter alone. And that's with amortization still flowing through. As VMware amortization tapers — intangibles already declined from $36.4B to $28.3B in one year — GAAP EPS will mechanically increase even without any operational improvement.

The forward EPS of $19.49 assumes: 1. Continued revenue growth (supported by AI demand and VMware cross-selling) 2. Further margin expansion (already happening — 69.5% gross margin, up from 67.9%) 3. Declining amortization charges (intangibles declining ~$8B/year) 4. Debt reduction lowering interest expense (net debt down $12.5B in four quarters)

None of these assumptions are heroic. They're the mechanical output of trends already in motion. The bear is treating accounting normalization as if it's fantasy — which tells me the bear doesn't understand how acquisition accounting works.

And the PEG ratio of 0.47? The bear says "if the G is inflated, the PEG is artificially low." But the G isn't inflated — if anything, the TTM earnings are artificially depressed by acquisition charges, making the growth rate from TTM to forward understated. The real PEG is probably even lower than 0.47.

3. The Balance Sheet: Goodwill Is Not a Ticking Time Bomb¶

The bear devotes significant space to the $97.8B goodwill and negative tangible book value, comparing AVGO to "Time Warner" and "GE Capital." This is apples-to-oranges reasoning of the worst kind.

Goodwill impairment occurs when acquired assets underperform their acquisition-price assumptions. VMware is not underperforming — it's exceeding expectations: - Gross margins expanding (67.9% → 69.5%) - Software segment contributing high-margin recurring revenue - Operating income nearly doubling YoY ($5.92B → $10.86B in Q2) - Integration delivering operational leverage visible in every metric

The bear says "if VMware integration stumbles..." — but it hasn't stumbled. It's accelerating. Every quarter of improving margins and growing cash flow makes impairment less likely, not more. The bear is asking us to price in a scenario that the data is actively disproving.

As for negative tangible book value: This is a complete non-issue for a company generating $27B+ in annual free cash flow. Tangible book value is an accounting construct that matters for banks and insurance companies — not for IP-driven semiconductor and software companies. The market doesn't value Broadcom based on its book value; it values it based on cash flow generation, and that cash flow is accelerating.

The bear's comparison to Time Warner and GE Capital is particularly egregious. Time Warner's acquisition of AOL failed because the business model was broken — dial-up internet was dying. GE Capital failed because of leverage and liquidity risk in financial assets. Broadcom's situation is nothing like either: the acquired assets (VMware's software franchise) are growing, the end markets (AI, cloud, enterprise) are expanding, and the cash flow is unambiguous.

4. The Technical Picture: The Bear Is Confusing Consolidation with Exhaustion¶

The bear makes several technical arguments that sound sophisticated but don't hold up to scrutiny.

ADX at 16.05: The Bear Misunderstands the Indicator¶

The bear says "if a 15% rally in a week can't push ADX above 20, what does that tell you about the quality of the buying?" Here's what it tells me: ADX measures 14-period directional movement, and AVGO spent the prior 8 weeks in a $360-$400 range. The ADX was crushed by that consolidation, and it takes time for a 14-period indicator to reflect a new directional move.

The bear is essentially saying: "Because AVGO was range-bound for two months, the current rally can't be real." That's circular reasoning. The range-bound period is why ADX is low, and the rally is what's causing ADX to rise. ADX went from 10.77 to 16.05 in four sessions — a 49% increase. If that trajectory continues, ADX crosses 20 within 3-4 sessions and 25 within a week. The indicator is catching up to reality, not denying it.

TD-9: The Bear's Selective Framing¶

The bear emphasizes the daily TD-9 at -7 and the monthly at -5, but conveniently ignores the weekly TD-9 at just -3. The weekly timeframe carries the highest structural weight, and it's barely begun a sell setup. This means the primary trend is not exhausted — only the short-term leg might be.

The bear also frames TD-9 completion as inevitable: "if the rally continues straight-line for two more sessions." But the report's own scenario analysis assigns 45% probability to a pullback/consolidation — which would reset the daily count. The bear is cherry-picking the 40% bullish continuation scenario's risk while ignoring that a consolidation (45% probability) would actually be healthy for the setup.

Volume: Normal, Not Concerning¶

The bear points to declining volume after August 4 (29.6M → 16.9M → 13.9M → 14.7M) as "breakout fade behavior." This is a misreading of normal market dynamics. The August 4 gap was a catalyst-driven event — likely related to news flow or positioning unwinds. Gap days always see elevated volume. The subsequent sessions at 14-17M are normal trading volume for AVGO (the report notes ~17M as average).

More importantly, price continued to rise on lower volume — from $418.16 to $427.76 over three sessions. Rising prices on declining volume is actually a sign of conviction, not weakness. It means sellers aren't showing up — there's no distribution pressure. If the bear were right about institutional selling, we'd see volume expand on down days. Instead, the stock grind higher.

The June Crash Comparison: Context Matters¶

The bear draws a direct parallel to the June crash ($494 → $370). But the contexts are fundamentally different:

Factor June Setup Current Setup
Prior move Parabolic spike to $494 (all-time high) Recovery from $370 low (well below ATH of $495)
RSI Likely >70 (overbought) 64.86 (below overbought)
MACD Histogram Unknown, but likely contracting after spike +5.36 and expanding
SuperTrend alignment Unknown UP on all 3 timeframes
Fundamental backdrop Pre-VMware synergy realization Post-VMware with 48% revenue growth and expanding margins

The June spike was a speculative blowoff at all-time highs. The current rally is a fundamental re-rating from a $370 base, supported by the strongest quarterly results in the company's history. These are not comparable setups, and the bear knows it.

5. The AI Moat: The Bear Doesn't Understand Broadcom's Business Model¶

The bear raises four challenges to the AI moat. Let me address each:

"Custom ASICs are a double-edged sword — Google could in-source"¶

The bear claims "Broadcom is a manufacturing partner, not the IP owner." This betrays a fundamental misunderstanding of Broadcom's custom silicon business. Broadcom doesn't just manufacture Google's TPUs — Broadcom co-designs them. The IP is jointly developed, and Broadcom contributes critical expertise in SerDes, memory interfaces, and advanced packaging that Google doesn't possess in-house.

The switching costs for a hyperscaler to replace Broadcom as a custom silicon partner are enormous — not just in redesigning chips, but in retooling manufacturing relationships, validation processes, and supply chains. Google has been working with Broadcom for multiple TPU generations. The relationship has deepened, not weakened, over time.

"AMD acquired an AI chip startup — competition is intensifying"¶

AMD buying a startup is a desperation move, not a competitive threat. AMD's 6% post-earnings decline shows the market's verdict on their AI strategy. Meanwhile, Broadcom is already generating revenue from custom AI silicon at scale — not acquiring startups to try to catch up. The bear is equating intent with capability. AMD intends to compete in AI; Broadcom is already winning in AI.

"The 'AI bubble survivor' narrative is a bubble tell"¶

The bear calls the Barchart headline a contrarian indicator and invokes 2000-2001. This is a superficial comparison. In 2000, companies with no revenue and no profits were valued at billions. Broadcom has: - $63.9B in annual revenue (FY2025) - $23.1B in net income (FY2025) - $27.2B in free cash flow (TTM) - 49% operating margins

This isn't a bubble stock. It's one of the most profitable companies in human history. The "survivor" narrative isn't a contrarian indicator — it's a factual description of a company with diversified revenue, real cash flow, and structural advantages.

"Customer concentration risk"¶

The bear raises customer concentration as if it's a revelation. This has been a known characteristic of Broadcom's business model for years. The market has been pricing this risk since long before the current rally. And the VMware acquisition specifically addressed this concern by diversifying into enterprise software with a broad customer base. The software segment now provides a revenue cushion that didn't exist before.

6. Macro Risk: The Bear Is Double-Counting¶

The bear raises two macro concerns — higher rates and trade tensions — and treats them as additive risks. In reality, they're partially offsetting:

Higher rates (86% probability of no cuts in 2026): Yes, this keeps discount rates elevated. But the bear ignores that AVGO's forward P/E of 22x is already reflective of a higher-rate environment. The stock has been de-rated from its 2021 highs. At 22x forward earnings with 48% revenue growth, the valuation already embeds significant rate risk. Trade tensions (6% resolution probability): The bear says "the probability of resolution is declining, which means the probability of escalation is rising." This is a false binary. The probability of resolution by August 31 is 6% — but that doesn't mean escalation is imminent. It means the status quo persists. The 8% recession probability is the most bullish macro signal in the entire analysis. The bear dismisses it, but an 8% recession probability means a 92% probability of no recession. In a non-recessionary environment with AI capex accelerating, AVGO's end markets are structurally supported.

The realistic risk/reward into earnings is not 5-10% upside vs. 15-30% downside. It's 15-20% upside on a beat-and-raise vs. 5-10% downside on a conservative guide — because the fundamental momentum is so strong that even a "conservative" guide would still show exceptional growth.

1. The Annualized Q2 EPS Math: The Bear's Smartest Argument, and Why It Still Fails¶

This is the bear's single strongest argument, and I want to treat it with the respect it deserves. But here's why it fails:

Quarter EPS Sequential Change
Q2 FY2025 $1.03 —

Q4 FY2025 $1.50 +$0.20
Q1 FY2026 $1.50 flat
Q2 FY2026 $1.91 +$0.41

EPS grew 85% YoY in Q2. But more importantly, the sequential acceleration from Q1 to Q2 ($0.41) was nearly double the prior sequential jump ($0.20-$0.27). None of these require "perfection." They require the continuation of existing trends. The bear's argument is essentially: "These trends will stop because trends always stop." That's not analysis — that's a tautology.

5. The Earnings Risk/Reward: The Bear's Framework Is Backward¶

The bear argues the 15.5% pre-earnings rally has "already priced in a beat-and-raise." This reveals a fundamental misunderstanding of how semiconductor stocks trade into earnings.

Semiconductor stocks routinely rally into earnings because of positioning, not because the beat is "priced in." The rally reflects: - Short covering (AVGO has significant short interest given its $2T market cap and high beta) - Options market-making hedging (dealers buying shares to hedge call positions) - Momentum and trend-following inflows (systematic strategies that buy strength) - Fundamental accumulation by institutions who want exposure before the catalyst

None of this means the beat is "priced in." It means the market is positioning for the beat. If the beat materializes, the positioning unwinds into higher prices as short sellers cover and options dealers re-hedge. If the beat doesn't materialize, the positioning unwinds into lower prices. The rally into earnings is a lever, not a ceiling.

The bear asks: "What surprise can move AVGO higher?" Here are several: 1. AI revenue acceleration beyond expectations — the bull thesis is still being proven, not fully priced 2. VMware cross-selling metrics — specific data on attach rates and customer expansion 3. Custom silicon design wins — new hyperscaler partnerships beyond Google and Meta 4. Margin guidance above consensus — the operating leverage story has room to surprise 5. Capital return acceleration — increased buyback authorization or special dividend

The bear claims "the only surprises available are negative ones." This is demonstrably false. A $2T company growing revenue 48% has multiple vectors for positive surprise, and the bear's failure to identify any of them reveals the poverty of their analysis — not the poverty of the opportunity.

6. The "Cycles Always Turn" Argument: True, But Timing Is Everything¶

The bear's final argument is their most philosophical: "All semiconductor cycles turn. The question is when, not if."

This is true. It's also true that the sun will eventually expand and consume the Earth. The question is: is it useful information for a decision I need to make today?

The semiconductor cycle is not a clock with a fixed period. It's driven by end-market demand, capacity utilization, and innovation cycles. The current AI infrastructure build-out is in its early innings: - Hyperscaler capex is accelerating, not plateauing - AI model sizes are doubling every 6-9 months, requiring exponentially more compute - Custom silicon adoption is in its first or second generation — multiple design cycles ahead - Power infrastructure deals (Constellation's 920 MW) signal multi-year data center buildouts

The bear invokes the 2018-2019 semiconductor downturn as a cautionary tale. But that downturn was driven by: - A memory oversupply cycle (not applicable to AVGO, which has minimal memory exposure) - The US-China trade war escalation (a known risk, already priced) - A smartphone demand decline (AVGO's wireless exposure is a smaller part of the mix post-VMware)

The current environment bears no resemblance to 2018-2019. AI demand is a secular, multi-year trend — not a cyclical peak. The bear is applying a cyclical framework to a structural growth story.

And here's the ultimate irony: even if the cycle turns in 12-18 months, the bear's recommendation to "wait for $370-380" assumes the cycle turns now. The bear is making a timing call dressed up as a risk management argument.

7. The Sentiment "Saturation" Argument: The Bear Doesn't Understand How Bull Markets Work¶

The bear says "when everyone is bullish, there's no marginal buyer." This is the most pernicious myth in market analysis, and it's been disproven repeatedly throughout market history.

Bull markets are defined by broad consensus. The S&P 500's bull run from 2009-2020 saw persistent bullish sentiment for years. The AI bull market of 2023-2025 saw NVIDIA rise 10x despite near-universal bullishness at every step. The notion that "everyone being bullish" marks a top is a contrarian superstition, not an evidence-based principle.

What actually marks a top is divergence — when fundamentals deteriorate while sentiment remains bullish. That's not happening here. The fundamentals are accelerating: - Revenue growth: accelerating (48% YoY) - Margin expansion: accelerating (69.5% gross margin, up 160bps YoY) - FCF growth: accelerating (60% YoY) - Deleveraging: accelerating ($12.5B reduction in four quarters)

When fundamentals and sentiment are aligned and both are improving, that's not a top. That's a trend.

The bear's options flow analysis is also wrong. Selling 440c/445c credit spreads is a neutral-to-bullish strategy (it profits if the stock stays below $440, which is only $12 above current price). Selling 405p/400p put ratio spreads is explicitly bullish (it provides downside support and profits if the stock holds above $405). The options market is positioning for AVGO to trade in the $405-$440 range — which is bullish, not bearish. The bear is misreading the flow.

The "You Agreed With Me" Trick: Hold ≠ Sell¶

There is a universe of difference between three positions:

  1. "Sell at $428" — the bear's actual recommendation
  2. "Don't chase at $428; hold existing longs with a stop at $376" — my actual recommendation
  3. "Buy aggressively at $428" — a position I never took

The bear is collapsing positions 1 and 2 into the same thing. They are not the same thing. "Hold with a stop" is a bullish position. It means: maintain exposure to the upside, participate in the earnings catalyst, and only exit if the structural trend breaks. "Sell" means: exit now, lock in the decline, and re-enter lower. These are opposite trades with opposite P&L outcomes.

If AVGO reports a beat-and-raise and the stock gaps to $460, the bear's "sell at $428" recommendation costs you $32 per share. My "hold with a stop at $376" recommendation captures that $32. If AVGO misses and the stock drops to $380, my stop at $376 protects the downside while the bear's "sell at $428" looks prescient. The risk profiles are completely different. The bear is equating "prudent entry management" with "bearish positioning" — and that's dishonest.

Saying "don't chase an overextended short-term rally" is standard risk management that every disciplined bull employs. Warren Buffett doesn't buy Berkshire at all-time highs either. That doesn't make him a bear on Berkshire.

The EPS Data: I'll Own the Error, But the Bear's Math Proves My Point¶

The bear caught me using an estimate for Q3 FY2025 EPS. Fair — I should have flagged that more clearly. But let me turn this around, because the bear's own analysis inadvertently confirms the bull thesis.

Let me do the math the bear refuses to do. FY2025 full-year EPS was $4.77. Q1 FY2026 was $1.50. Q2 FY2026 was $1.91. The second half of FY2025 (Q3 + Q4) produced $4.77 minus whatever Q1 and Q2 FY2025 produced. We know Q2 FY2025 was $1.03. And FY2027, with another year of amortization tailwind, debt reduction, and AI revenue compounding? The $19.49 forward estimate starts looking a lot more reasonable when you extend the horizon beyond a single annualized quarter.

The bear's "56x P/E" is a moment in time. The forward P/E of 22x is a trajectory. The difference matters enormously for a company whose earnings are mechanically inflecting upward due to accounting normalization and operational leverage.

Quarter Revenue YoY Growth

1. The "Hold = Sell" Trick: The Bear's Entire Case Rests on a Logical Fallacy¶

The bear's closing argument leans heavily on the claim that my "don't chase at $428" constitutes agreement with their "sell at $428." This is the rhetorical foundation of their entire closing, and it's false.

I'm not telling you the housing market is a bubble. I'm not telling you houses are overpriced. I'm telling you to practice discipline in your entry timing. That's risk management, not a bearish call.

The bear wants to erase the distinction between three fundamentally different positions:

Position What You Do P&L If AVGO Goes to $460 P&L If AVGO Goes to $380
Bull (mine): Hold with stop at $376 Maintain exposure +$32/share Exit at $376, -$52/share
Bear (theirs): Sell at $428 Exit completely $0 (missed gain) +$0 (avoided loss)

The bear is collapsing rows 1 and 2 into the same thing. They are not the same. My position captures the upside scenario that the bear's position forfeits. The bear's position protects against the downside scenario that my stop manages but doesn't fully eliminate. These are different trades with different expected values.

2. The EPS Math: I'll Be More Precise, and the Bull Case Gets Stronger¶

The bear's strongest point in this entire debate is catching my $7.71 FY2026 EPS estimate and noting that it implies a 55x P/E. I owe a more precise response than I gave previously, because this is the crux of the valuation debate.

Here's the key question: What does the $19.49 "forward EPS" actually represent?

The fundamental data lists "Forward EPS: $19.49" and "Forward PE: 21.95." This is calculated as $427.76 / $19.49 = 21.95. The $19.49 is a consensus forward estimate — and here's what the bear either doesn't understand or deliberately obscures:

Forward EPS estimates in financial databases typically reflect the next twelve months (NTM) of consensus analyst expectations, which increasingly use adjusted/non-GAAP figures for companies with significant acquisition accounting. Broadcom's GAAP EPS is heavily depressed by amortization of acquired intangibles — a non-cash charge that analysts routinely adjust for when modeling forward earnings.

Here's why this matters. In Q2 FY2026 alone, Broadcom recorded roughly $700M+ in amortization of acquired intangibles (annualized ~$2.8B based on the intangible balance declining from $36.4B to $28.3B). That's approximately $0.60 per share per quarter in non-cash charges that depress GAAP EPS but represent no economic outflow.

If we add back just the intangible amortization to my $7.71 FY2026 GAAP EPS estimate: - Adjusted FY2026 EPS ≈ $7.71 + ~$2.80B amortization / 4.68B shares ≈ $7.71 + $0.60 = $8.31

Still not $19.49? Correct — because the $19.49 likely represents a multi-year forward or normalized estimate that accounts for: 1. Full amortization runoff (intangibles declining from $28B toward ~$15-18B over 2-3 years) 2. Full VMware synergy realization (currently in early phases) 3. Continued AI-driven revenue growth 4. Debt reduction lowering interest expense 5. Share buyback reducing the share count

The $19.49 isn't a one-year jump from $7.71. It's a normalized earnings figure that the stock will grow into over 2-3 years. And here's the critical insight: the trajectory from $7.71 (FY2026 GAAP) toward $19.49 (normalized) is driven by mechanical, predictable forces — not heroic assumptions.

Let me quantify the mechanical tailwinds:

Driver Annual Impact Mechanism
Amortization decline ~$0.60/share/year Intangibles declining ~$8B/year
Interest expense reduction ~$0.15/share/year Net debt declining ~$3B/quarter
Revenue growth at 25% ~$1.50-2.00/share/year Operating leverage on incremental revenue
Buyback impact ~$0.20-0.30/share/year $6-8B annual buybacks reducing share count
Total annual EPS growth from mechanics alone ~$2.45-3.05/share/year Does not require any acceleration

Starting from a FY2026 base of ~$7.71 and adding ~$2.75/share/year in mechanical growth, you reach ~$13.2 by FY2028 and ~$16.0 by FY2029 — even without any AI-driven acceleration beyond current rates. The $19.49 becomes achievable within 3-4 years on purely mechanical trends.

The bear's error is treating the gap between $7.71 and $19.49 as a single-year leap requiring 153% growth. It's not. It's a 3-4 year normalization process driven by accounting mechanics, debt reduction, and baseline growth. The stock at $427.76 is trading at ~28x my FY2028 EPS estimate of ~$15 — which is an attractive multiple for a company with AVGO's market position and growth profile.

The bear calls the $19.49 "fantasy math." I call it the mechanical output of trends already visible in the financial statements. The difference is that I've done the decomposition and the bear hasn't.

3. Revenue Deceleration: The Bear Is Right About the Math, Wrong About the Implication¶

I'll concede the mechanical point: YoY revenue growth will compress from 48% to 28-38% as VMware comps lap. The bear is correct that this is arithmetic, not opinion.

But here's what the bear doesn't acknowledge: revenue growth deceleration from 48% to 30% is not the same as revenue growth deceleration from 30% to 12%. The first is a mechanical comp effect. The second would indicate a genuine demand slowdown.

At 30% YoY revenue growth with expanding gross margins (69.5% and rising) and sub-1% CapEx intensity, AVGO's free cash flow will continue to grow even as the headline growth rate compresses. Here's why:

Operating leverage compounds even as revenue growth decelerates. If revenue grows 30% YoY (from ~$85B to ~$110B) but operating expenses grow only 15% (due to VMware integration efficiencies and software's lower incremental cost), operating income grows faster than revenue. And since amortization is declining as a percentage of revenue, GAAP earnings grow even faster than operating income.

The bear asks: "Will the market continue paying 55-70x earnings for a company growing 25-30%?" The answer is: the market won't need to, because the denominator (earnings) is growing faster than the numerator (price). If AVGO's stock price is flat for two years while EPS grows from $7.71 to $13+, the P/E mechanically compresses from 55x to 33x — without any price decline.

This is the fundamental difference between a "value trap" (where earnings stagnate and the multiple never compresses) and a "growth re-rating" (where earnings catch up to the price). AVGO is unambiguously the latter.

Bear Researcher¶

Bear Analyst: # The Bear Case for AVGO: A $2 Trillion House of Cards on a Fault Line

Let me be direct: my bull colleague has delivered an eloquent defense of AVGO's fundamentals, and I'll concede that the numbers are impressive on the surface. But that's precisely the problem — on the surface. When you scratch beneath the glossy revenue growth figures and the carefully curated forward multiples, you find a stock priced for perfection in a market showing cracks, supported by technicals that are screaming exhaustion, and carrying balance sheet risks that could cascade if any of the bullish assumptions unravel.

Let me dismantle the bull case piece by piece.

The "48% Growth" Illusion: Acquisition Accounting Masquerading as Organic Strength¶

My colleague trumpets the 48% YoY revenue growth in Q2 FY2026 as evidence of a company "growing like a startup." Let me inject some reality here.

A significant portion of that growth is the VMware acquisition anniversary effect, not organic momentum. VMware closed in November 2023. Q2 FY2025 (April 2025) was the first full quarter that included a complete year-over-year VMware comparison. The 48% growth my colleague celebrates is largely the product of lapping partial VMware contributions, not runaway demand for Broadcom's core franchises.

Here's the critical question the bull conveniently sidesteps: What's the organic growth rate ex-VMware? If you strip out the software segment's acquisition-driven contribution, the semiconductor business — which includes all those AI ASICs and networking chips the bulls love to hype — is growing at a far more modest pace. The sequential growth from Q1 to Q2 FY2026 ($19.31B to $22.19B) is roughly 15%, which is strong but not the "accelerating startup growth" narrative being sold.

And here's the ugly comp reality: By Q3 and Q4 FY2026, AVGO will be lapping full VMware contributions. The YoY growth rate will mechanically compress. The market has priced in continued 40%+ growth. When the print shows 20-25%, what happens to that forward P/E of 22x? It expands to 30x+ overnight on revised estimates, and the stock corrects.

The Valuation Mirage: Forward P/E and PEG Are Fantasy Math¶

My colleague calls the forward P/E of 21.95 and PEG of 0.47 "the critical valuation insight the bears miss." I'd argue it's the critical delusion the bulls are selling.

The forward P/E of 21.95 is based on forward EPS of $19.49. Let's do some math. TTM EPS is $6.12. To get to $19.49, you're talking about a 218% increase in earnings. The bull will say "but Q2 FY2026 EPS was $1.91 vs. $1.03 — that's 85% growth!" Fine. But even annualizing Q2's $1.91 gives you roughly $7.64 per quarter — call it $8B+ in net income per quarter, or ~$32B annually. On 4.68B shares, that's about $6.84 per share annualized from the most recent quarter.

Wait — that's barely above the TTM EPS of $6.12. So where does $19.49 come from? It assumes massive continued acceleration and the full runoff of VMware amortization impacts. This is the kind of forward estimate that gets revised down aggressively when reality meets spreadsheet optimism. If forward EPS comes in at, say, $12-14 instead of $19.49 (still excellent growth!), your "attractive" forward P/E balloons to 30-36x.

And the PEG ratio of 0.47? A PEG below 1.0 is only meaningful if the growth rate (G) is sustainable and real. If the "G" is inflated by acquisition accounting and one-time integration synergies that can't be replicated, then the PEG is artificially low. The market isn't mispricing AVGO at a PEG of 0.47 — the PEG is mispricing itself.

Meanwhile, the TTM P/E of 69.90 and Price-to-Book of 23.21 are the real, verifiable valuation metrics. At 70x trailing earnings, you're paying a premium that leaves zero margin for error.

The Balance Sheet: A $97.8 Billion Ghost on the Books¶

My colleague breezes past the balance sheet with "debt is declining" and "deleveraging on track." Let me shine some light on what's lurking there.

Goodwill of $97.8 billion represents 54.6% of total assets. Add intangibles of $28.3B, and you have $126.1 billion — over 70% of the asset base — in acquired intangible value. If VMware integration stumbles, if AI spending slows and margins compress, if any of the bullish assumptions prove wrong, we're looking at impairment charges that would make the FY2024 net income dip ($5.9B) look like a rounding error.

Tangible book value is negative $38.4 billion. My colleague dismisses this as "common for serial acquirers." True — it's also common for serial acquirers to face existential crises when acquisitions underperform. Remember Time Warner? Remember GE Capital? "Common" doesn't mean "safe."

Total debt is still $64.9 billion, and while net debt has declined to $45.3B, that's still a massive obligation carrying ~$3.1B in annual interest expense. Yes, FCF covers it comfortably today. But semiconductor cycles are brutal, and FCF can compress with shocking speed when end markets roll over. The notion that current FCF levels are permanent is a bull market conceit.

The Technical Picture: My Colleague Is Whistling Past the Graveyard¶

Let me address the technical counterarguments directly, because my colleague's dismissals are dangerously superficial.

If a 15% rally in a week can't push ADX above 20, what does that tell you about the quality of the buying?

The SuperTrend being "UP on all timeframes" is the only structural bull signal here, and I'll grant it carries weight. But SuperTrend is a trend-following system that generates false signals in range-bound or choppy markets — exactly the environment AVGO was in for two months before this rally. The daily SuperTrend flipped UP during the rally, which is almost tautological. The weekly stop at $334.85 is so far below current price ($427.76) that it provides no meaningful short-term risk management. It's a 27% drawdown away.

TD-9 at -7: "It can reset!"¶

Yes, it can reset. It also can complete at -9, which is the more probable outcome if the rally continues straight-line for two more sessions. My colleague's argument is essentially: "Don't worry about the exhaustion signal because maybe it won't trigger." That's not risk management — that's hope.

And let's note the monthly TD-9 at -5. The monthly timeframe carries structural weight. A sell-setup building on the monthly chart means the DeMark framework is flagging potential trend exhaustion, not just a short-term pullback. My colleague conveniently ignores this.

That's a 50% decline in volume from the breakout day. The institutions bought on August 4, and then... nobody followed. This is classic breakout fade behavior, not the start of a sustained institutional accumulation phase.
